12	
13	\begin{figure*}[t!]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
16	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (red for drifting, blue for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}
17	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
18	\end{figure*}
19	We focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}).
20	Figure~\ref{fig:framework} summarizes the pipeline: recursive generation chains (\S\ref{sec:generation}), feature extraction with DDR partitioning (\S\ref{sec:features}), and leave-one-generator-out evaluation (\S\ref{sec:evaluation}).
21	
22	\looseness=-1 As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA \cite{qlora} (Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation) classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.
23	
24	Our contributions:
25	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
26	    \item \textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into two primary response zones---drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) vs.\ near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---plus one intermediate feature ($d{=}0.46$). Where prior work documents \emph{what converges} \cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \emph{what is approximately preserved}, an empirical regularity with analytical utility for depth classification without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.
27	    \item \textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B; exploratory evidence extends to 14B, Appendix~\ref{app:9gen-scale}), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.
28	    \item \textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.
29	\end{enumerate}
30

(a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\n\\looseness=-1 These span a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B) across five architectures; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8) is a limitation mitigated by LOFO (F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same seed pool serves as gen-0 for all generators, controlling for input content; in LOGO evaluation this creates a potential train--test overlap for gen-0, addressed by our binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class LOGO yields macro F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50, with depths 2--4 near-indistinguishable (gen-2/gen-3 per-class F1\\,$<$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition (LOFO confirms both feature groups contribute independently; Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), covering the NLL distribution's location, spread, extremes, and tail structure, plus two reference-free surface diversity measures; six additional NLL statistics were rejected for redundancy ($|r| > 0.65$, $\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\n\\looseness=-1 A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nCross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 We evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\n\\looseness=-1 We use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); hyperparameter sensitivity (max\\_depth sweep) shows F1 variation $\\leq$2.0\\,pp, confirming discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",

{

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "newString": "    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Depth profiling pipeline. (a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1 An estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \\cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \\emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nBase models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.\nWe find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \\emph{location} drifts with depth while \\emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth profiling} (coarse ordinal classification into gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy\\footnote{Throughout, ``entropy'' denotes per-token NLL under the reference model (Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ more stable (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.46$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component complements location drift for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (red for drifting, blue for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\nWe focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:framework} summarizes the pipeline: recursive generation chains (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), feature extraction with DDR partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:features}), and leave-one-generator-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA \\cite{qlora} (Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation) classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into two primary response zones---drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) vs.\\ near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---plus one intermediate feature ($d{=}0.46$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges} \\cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \\emph{what is approximately preserved}, an empirical regularity with analytical utility for depth classification without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B; exploratory evidence extends to 14B, Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

          "     \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.",

          "+\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}",
          "+\\caption{Depth profiling pipeline. (a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}",

```text
56	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
57	\label{sec:results-differential}
58	
59	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
60	\looseness=-1 For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
61	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$ ($k$-means silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$; exhaustive split enumeration ranks this partition first; independent replication in all eight generators; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
62	\looseness=-1 Recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical~\citep{markovian_chains}, predicting location drift with shape invariance (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
63	
64	\looseness=-1 Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals the dichotomy: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat (per-feature distributions: Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}; qualitative chain example: Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative}).
65	The partition is binary ($k{=}2$; silhouette\,=\,0.660), with entropy std as the sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.46$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
70	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Drifting features (red; $|\rho| > 0.60$, effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) shift monotonically with generation depth; near-stationary features (blue; $d \leq 0.10$) are approximately preserved; entropy std (purple; $d{=}0.46$) is intermediate. Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values.}
71	\label{fig:feature-drift}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	\looseness=-1 Pooled Spearman correlations corroborate the split: drifting features show $|\rho| > 0.60$ (hapax ratio $-0.64$, TTR $-0.62$, entropy mean $-0.60$) vs.\ $|\rho| \leq 0.16$ for entropy max and tail decay; entropy min reaches $|\rho|{=}0.40$ due to its gen-0/gen-1 boundary sensitivity (discussed below)---a persistent magnitude gap defining the DDR partition (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}).
75	
76	\paragraph{Quantification.}
77	\looseness=-1 Cohen's $d$ shows a ${\geq}8{\times}$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) and near-stationary ($d < 0.10$); KS, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$ corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
78	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1; tail decay passes two one-sided tests (TOST) equivalence (Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
79	\paragraph{Validation.}
80	\looseness=-1 Each hold-out generator independently recovers the identical partition (silhouette 0.505--0.760); TOST confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8) and entropy max (6/8); permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$). Per-feature convergence dynamics (Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}), per-family DDR consistency (Appendix~\ref{app:family-ddr}), and causal feature analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-causality}) appear in Appendices~\ref{app:ddr-validation}--\ref{app:convergence}.
81	
82	\begin{figure}[t]
83	\centering
84	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_convergence_dynamics.pdf}
85	\caption{Feature convergence dynamics. Drifting features (red): $|d| > 1$ by gen-2+; near-stationary features (blue): $|d| < 0.25$ at all depths, confirming shape stability persists across chain lengths.}
86	\label{fig:convergence}
87	\end{figure}
88	
89	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
90	\looseness=-1 The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified cross-validation) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
91	Group ablation confirms complementarity: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only 0.697 vs.\ all-7 0.700)---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
92	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
93	
94	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
95	\looseness=-1 Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) yield F1 spread of only 0.013 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}); same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.2\,pp, but the DDR partition is preserved.
96	
97	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
98	\looseness=-1 All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
99	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), including cross-temperature transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}); random word replacement at 10\% degrades binary F1 by only 8\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
100	
101	
102	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
103	\label{sec:results-baselines}
104	
105	\begin{figure}[t]
```

The layout hasn't changed — moving the convergence figure definition earlier didn't change its placement. Pages 5-8 look identical to before. The convergence figure is still on page 7 alongside the baseline comparison figure.

This is because LaTeX float placement is determined by where there's room, not just source order. Since the figure definition was moved but the surrounding text hasn't changed much, LaTeX still places it on page 7.

Let me try a more aggressive approach: use `[b]` placement for the convergence figure instead of `[t]`, or use `\FloatBarrier` to force figures to appear before certain text boundaries. Or I could swap which column each figure goes in.


Actually, I need to move the convergence figure even earlier — right after the formal definition paragraph, before the DDR strip figure. Looking at my current edit, I placed it before the Validation paragraph but it's still coming after the DDR strip figure. Moving it further up in the source should give LaTeX more opportunity to place it earlier in the document.

The real constraint here is that page 6 already has both top positions filled — Table 2 on the left and Figure 4 (DDR strip) on the right — so the convergence figure can't fit there if it's also a single-column `[t]` figure. On page 5, the right column has Figure 3 at the top, so there might be space in the left column if I move the convergence figure earlier in the source order.

Since LaTeX processes floats sequentially, I need to get the convergence figure into the queue before the DDR strip and Table 2. I could either change its placement to `[b]` (bottom) to avoid competing for top slots, or move its definition much earlier — ideally to the start of the DDR subsection — so it gets processed sooner and lands on an earlier page.

```text
55	
56	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
57	\label{sec:results-differential}
58	
59	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
60	\looseness=-1 For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
61	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$ ($k$-means silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$; exhaustive split enumeration ranks this partition first; independent replication in all eight generators; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
62	\looseness=-1 Recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical~\citep{markovian_chains}, predicting location drift with shape invariance (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
63	
64	\looseness=-1 Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals the dichotomy: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat (per-feature distributions: Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}; qualitative chain example: Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative}).
65	The partition is binary ($k{=}2$; silhouette\,=\,0.660), with entropy std as the sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.46$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
70	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Drifting features (red; $|\rho| > 0.60$, effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) shift monotonically with generation depth; near-stationary features (blue; $d \leq 0.10$) are approximately preserved; entropy std (purple; $d{=}0.46$) is intermediate. Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values.}
71	\label{fig:feature-drift}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	\looseness=-1 Pooled Spearman correlations corroborate the split: drifting features show $|\rho| > 0.60$ (hapax ratio $-0.64$, TTR $-0.62$, entropy mean $-0.60$) vs.\ $|\rho| \leq 0.16$ for entropy max and tail decay; entropy min reaches $|\rho|{=}0.40$ due to its gen-0/gen-1 boundary sensitivity (discussed below)---a persistent magnitude gap defining the DDR partition (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}).
75	
76	\paragraph{Quantification.}
77	\looseness=-1 Cohen's $d$ shows a ${\geq}8{\times}$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) and near-stationary ($d < 0.10$); KS, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$ corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
78	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1; tail decay passes two one-sided tests (TOST) equivalence (Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
79	\paragraph{Validation.}
80	\begin{figure}[t]
81	\centering
82	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_convergence_dynamics.pdf}
83	\caption{Feature convergence dynamics. Drifting features (red): $|d| > 1$ by gen-2+; near-stationary features (blue): $|d| < 0.25$ at all depths, confirming shape stability persists across chain lengths.}
84	\label{fig:convergence}
85	\end{figure}
86	
87	\looseness=-1 Each hold-out generator independently recovers the identical partition (silhouette 0.505--0.760); TOST confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8) and entropy max (6/8); permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$). Per-feature convergence dynamics (Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}), per-family DDR consistency (Appendix~\ref{app:family-ddr}), and causal feature analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-causality}) appear in Appendices~\ref{app:ddr-validation}--\ref{app:convergence}.
88	
89	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
90	\looseness=-1 The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified cross-validation) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
91	Group ablation confirms complementarity: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only 0.697 vs.\ all-7 0.700)---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
92	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
93	
94	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
```

Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).\nA scale effect emerges: smaller models achieve higher F1 (Pythia-2.8B: 0.724 vs.\\ Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (spread $\\leq$0.013; \\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nAn end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; $+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming additional depth signal in token-level representations; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (within-model; Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition as DDR's primary contribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound controls.}\n\\looseness=-1 Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.724 (vs.\\ 0.770; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}); independent-seed evaluation with disjoint pools (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}) confirms the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.\n\n\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\n\\looseness=-1 The gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point: depth-2 samples---the shallowest within the 2+ class---exhibit the highest error rate (48.4\\%), decreasing monotonically to 20.3\\% at depth-4 (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}), reflecting insufficient accumulated drift beyond the large gen-0$\\to$gen-1 distributional shift.\nError rates decrease monotonically at deeper generations ($\\leq$29.2\\% at gen-3+), confirming that DDR signal strengthens with chain length; Drifting features (red): $|d| > 1$ by gen-2+; near-stationary features (blue): $|d| < 0.25$ at all depths, confirming shape stability persists across chain lengths.}\n\\label{fig:convergence}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Formal definition.}\n\\looseness=-1 For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \\in \\{0,1,2{+}\\}$, we define its \\emph{depth sensitivity} as $\\text{DDR}(f) = |\\rho(\\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.\nA feature is classified as \\emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \\emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; the two-cluster structure is robust across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$ ($k$-means silhouette\\,=\\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$; exhaustive split enumeration ranks this partition first; independent replication in all eight generators; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\\looseness=-1 Recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical~\\citep{markovian_chains}, predicting location drift with shape invariance (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 Figure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals the dichotomy: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat (per-feature distributions: Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-distributions}; qualitative chain example: Appendix~\\ref{app:qualitative}).\nThe partition is binary ($k{=}2$; silhouette\\,=\\,0.660), with entropy std as the sole \\emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.46$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Drifting features (red; $|\\rho| > 0.60$, effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) shift monotonically with generation depth; near-stationary features (blue; $d \\leq 0.10$) are approximately preserved; entropy std (purple; $d{=}0.46$) is intermediate. Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Pooled Spearman correlations corroborate the split: drifting features show $|\\rho| > 0.60$ (hapax ratio $-0.64$, TTR $-0.62$, entropy mean $-0.60$) vs.\\ $|\\rho| \\leq 0.16$ for entropy max and tail decay; entropy min reaches $|\\rho|{=}0.40$ due to its gen-0/gen-1 boundary sensitivity (discussed below)---a persistent magnitude gap defining the DDR partition (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}).\n\n\\paragraph{Quantification.}\n\\looseness=-1 Cohen's $d$ shows a ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) and near-stationary ($d < 0.10$); KS, Glass's $\\Delta$, Cliff's $\\delta$ corroborate (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nEntropy min and tail decay are \\emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1; tail decay passes two one-sided tests (TOST) equivalence (Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 Each hold-out generator independently recovers the identical partition (silhouette 0.505--0.760); TOST confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8) and entropy max (6/8); permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$). Per-feature convergence dynamics (Figure~\\ref{fig:convergence}), per-family DDR consistency (Appendix~\\ref{app:family-ddr}), and causal feature analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-causality}) appear in Appendices~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}--\\ref{app:convergence}.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\n\\looseness=-1 The full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773, 10-fold stratified cross-validation) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table}).\nGroup ablation confirms complementarity: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only 0.697 vs.\\ all-7 0.700)---shape stability contributes to depth \\emph{grading}, not detection.\nTTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\n\\looseness=-1 Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) yield F1 spread of only 0.013 (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}); same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.2\\,pp, but the DDR partition is preserved.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}\n\\looseness=-1 All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\\,=\\,0.781; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nThe DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), including cross-temperature transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-temp}); random word replacement at 10\\% degrades binary F1 by only 8\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\\ inference cost (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M model) matches multi-detector ensembles (1.4B total) at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost. RTX 5090 timings.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled). 7-feature: one 410M model; 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B. QLoRA (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}) uses full token sequences (within-model 5-fold CV, not LOGO)---an information-asymmetric ceiling.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\nThe strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\\,=\\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.\n\n\\looseness=-1 The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble on binary F1 (0.705 vs.\\ 0.708; Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost, though the latter leads by 2.1\\,pp on 3-class F1 (0.795 vs.\\ 0.774); both surpass individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\\,pp.\nThe DDR decomposition explains why depth discrimination is achievable---location features carry the signal, shape features enable cross-generator transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion})---with a single 410M reference model; ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}).\nAmong individual detectors, LD-Score and Avg-LogProb best capture depth structure (binary F1\\,=\\,0.675 and 0.673); FastDetectGPT captures 3-class ordering (F1\\,=\\,0.660) but struggles with binary depth separation (F1\\,=\\,0.569); Binoculars is least informative (binary F1\\,=\\,0.539).\nConcurrent iterative-generation studies use different chain operators, precluding direct inclusion as depth baselines (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\n",

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1 An estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \\cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \\emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nBase models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.\nWe find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \\emph{location} drifts with depth while \\emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth profiling} (coarse ordinal classification into gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy\\footnote{Throughout, ``entropy'' denotes per-token NLL under the reference model (Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ more stable (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.46$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component complements location drift for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (red for drifting, blue for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\nWe focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:framework} summarizes the pipeline: recursive generation chains (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), feature extraction with DDR partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:features}), and leave-one-generator-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA \\cite{qlora} (Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation) classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into two primary response zones---drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) vs.\\ near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---plus one intermediate feature ($d{=}0.46$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges} \\cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \\emph{what is approximately preserved}, an empirical regularity with analytical utility for depth classification without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B; exploratory evidence extends to 14B, Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Depth profiling pipeline. (a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n",
        "numLines": 37,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 37
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "5a2cfbc9-c6ec-454c-81f9-e12c72fa3597",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{table}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (\\S\\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals entropy mean, TTR, and hapax ratio drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Measures} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{entropy\\_mean} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)} & Ref.\\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_std} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_max} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_min} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{tail\\_decay}   & Ref.\\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\\\\n\\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\n\\looseness=-1 These span a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B) across five architectures; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8) is a limitation mitigated by LOFO (F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same seed pool serves as gen-0 for all generators, controlling for input content; in LOGO evaluation this creates a potential train--test overlap for gen-0, addressed by our binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class LOGO yields macro F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50, with depths 2--4 near-indistinguishable (gen-2/gen-3 per-class F1\\,$<$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition (LOFO confirms both feature groups contribute independently; Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), covering the NLL distribution's location, spread, extremes, and tail structure, plus two reference-free surface diversity measures; six additional NLL statistics were rejected for redundancy ($|r| > 0.65$, $\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\n\\looseness=-1 A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nCross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 We evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\n\\looseness=-1 We use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); hyperparameter sensitivity (max\\_depth sweep) shows F1 variation $\\leq$2.0\\,pp, confirming discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",
        "numLines": 90,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 90
      }
    },

        "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Recursive generation produces a decomposable depth response---location features drift monotonically while shape features remain approximately preserved---enabling cross-generator depth classification (F1\\,=\\,0.694--0.762) under moderate sampling temperatures ($\\tau \\leq 0.7$).\nCross-model chains confirm generalization across five families (F1\\,=\\,0.830; ANOVA: depth explains ${>}48{\\times}$ more location-feature variance than shape-feature variance, generator identity ${<}1$\\%).\nThe decomposition is robust across decoding strategies, temperatures, prompt types, and reference models, with the DDR partition independently recovered by every held-out generator.\nQLoRA recovery (F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91, within-model) suggests tractable extension to instruct models; DDR connects depth profiling to corpus auditing and model collapse monitoring.\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_transfer_heatmap.pdf}
7	\caption{Cross-generator $8{\times}8$ transfer matrix (binary F1, gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Mean cross-generator F1\,=\,0.810 [95\% CI: 0.796, 0.822]; uniformly high off-diagonal values confirm depth signal transfers across five families without calibration.}
8	\label{fig:transfer-heatmap}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
12	\label{sec:results-classification}
13	
14	\looseness=-1 The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
15	This 3-class metric is inflated by shared gen-0 seeds (per-class gen-0 F1\,$\approx$\,0.94; \S\ref{sec:generation}); we therefore report \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) throughout, excluding gen-0 entirely.
16	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; std.\ dev.\ 0.028), with the primary difficulty at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
19	\looseness=-1 Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B; generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,1{,}000).}
20	Single-generator transfer confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact (mean transfer F1\,=\,0.810, Figure~\ref{fig:transfer-heatmap}; full $8{\times}8$ matrix: Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}; LOFO\,=\,0.686); analysis of variance (ANOVA) confirms generator identity contributes ${<}1$\% of feature variance ($\eta^2_{\text{gen}} \leq 0.009$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).
21	A scale effect emerges: smaller models achieve higher F1 (Pythia-2.8B: 0.724 vs.\ Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
22	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; $+$10.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming additional depth signal in token-level representations; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (within-model; Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition as DDR's primary contribution.
23	
24	\paragraph{Confound controls.}
25	\looseness=-1 Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770; Appendix~\ref{app:length}); independent-seed evaluation with disjoint pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) confirms the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
26	
27	\paragraph{Error analysis.}
28	\looseness=-1 The gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point: depth-2 samples---the shallowest within the 2+ class---exhibit the highest error rate (48.4\%), decreasing monotonically to 20.3\% at depth-4 (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}), reflecting insufficient accumulated drift beyond the large gen-0$\to$gen-1 distributional shift.
29	Error rates decrease monotonically at deeper generations ($\leq$29.2\% at gen-3+), confirming that DDR signal strengthens with chain length; the DDR decomposition is the primary analytical contribution.
30

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FwJKufwrX1GfensvoMk7zv`</summary>

```text
65	
66	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
67	\looseness=-1 For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
68	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$ ($k$-means silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$; exhaustive split enumeration ranks this partition first; independent replication in all eight generators; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
69	\looseness=-1 Recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical~\citep{markovian_chains}, predicting location drift with shape invariance (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
70	
71	\looseness=-1 Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals the dichotomy: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat (per-feature distributions: Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}; qualitative chain example: Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative}).
72	The partition is binary ($k{=}2$; silhouette\,=\,0.660), with entropy std as the sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.46$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
73	
74	\begin{figure}[t]
75	\centering
76	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
77	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Drifting features (red; $|\rho| > 0.60$, effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) shift monotonically with generation depth; near-stationary features (blue; $d \leq 0.10$) are approximately preserved; entropy std (purple; $d{=}0.46$) is intermediate. Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values.}
78	\label{fig:feature-drift}
79	\end{figure}
80	
81	\looseness=-1 Pooled Spearman correlations corroborate the split: drifting features show $|\rho| > 0.60$ (hapax ratio $-0.64$, TTR $-0.62$, entropy mean $-0.60$) vs.\ $|\rho| \leq 0.16$ for entropy max and tail decay; entropy min reaches $|\rho|{=}0.40$ due to its gen-0/gen-1 boundary sensitivity (discussed below)---a persistent magnitude gap defining the DDR partition (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}).
82	
83	\paragraph{Quantification.}
84	\looseness=-1 Cohen's $d$ shows a ${\geq}8{\times}$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) and near-stationary ($d < 0.10$); KS, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$ corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
85	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1; tail decay passes two one-sided tests (TOST) equivalence (Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
86	\paragraph{Validation.}
87	\looseness=-1 Each hold-out generator independently recovers the identical partition (silhouette 0.505--0.760); TOST confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8) and entropy max (6/8); permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$). Per-feature convergence dynamics (Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}), per-family DDR consistency (Appendix~\ref{app:family-ddr}), and causal feature analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-causality}) appear in Appendices~\ref{app:ddr-validation}--\ref{app:convergence}.
88	
89	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
90	\looseness=-1 The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified cross-validation) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
91	Group ablation confirms complementarity: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only 0.697 vs.\ all-7 0.700)---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
92	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
93	
94	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
95	\looseness=-1 Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) yield F1 spread of only 0.013 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}); same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.2\,pp, but the DDR partition is preserved.
96	
97	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
98	\looseness=-1 All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
99	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), including cross-temperature transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}); random word replacement at 10\% degrades binary F1 by only 8\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
100	
101	
102	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
103	\label{sec:results-baselines}
104	
105	\begin{figure}[t]
106	\centering
107	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
108	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M model) matches multi-detector ensembles (1.4B total) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost. RTX 5090 timings.}
109	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
110	\end{figure}
111	
112	\begin{table}[t]
113	\centering
114	\footnotesize
115	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled). 7-feature: one 410M model; 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B. QLoRA (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}) uses full token sequences (within-model 5-fold CV, not LOGO)---an information-asymmetric ceiling.}
116	\label{tab:baselines}
117	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
118	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
119	\toprule
120	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
121	\midrule
122	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
123	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
124	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
125	\midrule
126	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
127	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
128	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
129	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
130	\bottomrule
131	\end{tabular}}
132	\end{table}
133	
134	\looseness=-1 We next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
135	The strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\,=\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.
136	
137	\looseness=-1 The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble on binary F1 (0.705 vs.\ 0.708; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost, though the latter leads by 2.1\,pp on 3-class F1 (0.795 vs.\ 0.774); both surpass individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\,pp.
138	The DDR decomposition explains why depth discrimination is achievable---location features carry the signal, shape features enable cross-generator transfer (\S\ref{sec:discussion})---with a single 410M reference model; ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}).
139	Among individual detectors, LD-Score and Avg-LogProb best capture depth structure (binary F1\,=\,0.675 and 0.673); FastDetectGPT captures 3-class ordering (F1\,=\,0.660) but struggles with binary depth separation (F1\,=\,0.569); Binoculars is least informative (binary F1\,=\,0.539).
140	Concurrent iterative-generation studies use different chain operators, precluding direct inclusion as depth baselines (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).
141

Abstract 原文保持不动 —— 不要删改、截断、替换 Abstract 的任何一句话\n2. 在 Abstract 最后一句话的句号之前加一个 \\footnote{}，脚注标记（上标 ¹）挂在句末\n3. 整句 \"Code and data are available at ...\" 写在脚注里面，不要出现在 Abstract 正文中\n\n标准 LaTeX 模板：\n\n... We release our code and data at\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}}.\n\n❌ 错误（截断原句）：\n\nOur experiments\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{...}}.\n\n---\n\n请立刻检查并修复：\n\n1. 打开你的论文 Abstract 部分\n2. 确认最后一句话是完整的、未被修改的原始句子\n3. 如果句子被截断或改写了，恢复原文\n4. 按上面的模板把脚注挂在最后一句的句号前\n\n完成后请汇报：Abstract 最后一句话是什么？脚注格式是否已按上述标准修正？"

_source line 2140 · 2026-05-25T10:12:24.237Z_

[飞书用户 用户]: ⚠️ 紧急修正：匿名仓库脚注格式问题

之前发的匿名仓库链接规范有表述不清的地方，导致很多论文的 Abstract 最后一句话被截断或改写了。请立刻按以下标准修正。

---

问题描述：

很多论文现在的 Abstract 最后一句话写到一半就断了，或者被改成了不完整的句子。这是因为之前的模板让你把 "We release our code and data at..." 直接拼在了 Abstract 原文里，破坏了原来的句子结构。

正确做法：

1. Abstract 原文保持不动 —— 不要删改、截断、替换 Abstract 的任何一句话
2. 在 Abstract 最后一句话的句号之前加一个 \footnote{}，脚注标记（上标 ¹）挂在句末
3. 整句 "Code and data are available at ..." 写在脚注里面，不要出现在 Abstract 正文中

标准 LaTeX 模板：

... (Abstract 原来的最后一句话，一个字都不要改)\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{你的匿名仓库地址}.}.

具体示例：

假设你的 Abstract 最后一句原文是 "Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements across all benchmarks."

✅ 正确：

Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements across all benchmarks\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}.}.

❌ 错误（之前很多人的做法）：

Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements. We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}}.

❌ 错误（截断原句）：

Our experiments\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{...}}.

---

请立刻检查并修复：

1. 打开你的论文 Abstract 部分
2. 确认最后一句话是完整的、未被修改的原始句子
3. 如果句子被截断或改写了，恢复原文
4. 按上面的模板把脚注挂在最后一句的句号前

完成后请汇报：Abstract 最后一句话是什么？脚注格式是否已按上述标准修正？

Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). ### Tool result

_source line 2143 · 2026-05-25T10:12:28.818Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011F1joxE6DDyoGyRTn29Jur`</summary>

```text
1	% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As large language model (LLM) outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions is critical for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \emph{generation depth}: we introduce \emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.
4	We decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, negative log-likelihood (NLL) location features drift monotonically with depth (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.46$)---a ${\geq}8{\times}$ effect-size gap.
5	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape stability complements location drift for ordinal depth grading without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), cross-model binary F1\,=\,0.830, and 3-class F1\,=\,0.762 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation (gen-0 seeds shared across generators; \S\ref{sec:generation}).
6	Instruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models. We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/gen-depth-fingerprint}}.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```
